# The Zodiacal Chart of Letters
<note>original: "Varnanam Rashichakram"</note>

<note>The handwriting in the fourth row, which lists the vowels and consonants, is significantly faded and delicate. Some red ink annotations used to highlight the qualities are also quite thin.</note>

| Male | Female | Male | Female | Male | Female | Male | Female | Male | Female | Male | Female |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **Aries** | **Taurus** | **Gemini** | **Cancer** | **Leo** | **Virgo** | **Libra** | **Scorpio** | **Sagittarius** | **Capricorn** | **Aquarius** | **Pisces** |
| Movable | Fixed | Dual | Movable | Fixed | Dual | Movable | Fixed | Dual | Movable | Fixed | Dual |
| a, ā, i, ī,<br>u, ū, ṛ, ṝ | ḷ, ḹ, e, ai,<br>o, au, aṃ, aḥ | k, kh, g, gh, ṅ | c, ch, j, jh, ñ | ṭ, ṭh, ḍ, ḍh, ṇ | t, th, d, dh, n | p, ph, b, bh, m | y, r, l, v | ś, ṣ, s, h | kṣ | tr | jñ |
| **Fierce** | **Gentle** | **Fierce** | **Gentle** | **Fierce** | **Gentle** | **Fierce** | **Gentle** | **Fierce** | **Gentle** | **Fierce** | **Gentle** |

<note>
This chart functions as a mnemonic and diagnostic tool in Vedic astrology. It categorizes the signs by several attributes:
- **Gender:** Alternating between Male (<term>Purusha</term>) and Female (<term>Stri</term>).
- **Nature:** <term>Chara</term> (Movable/active), <term>Sthira</term> (Fixed/stable), and <term>Dvisvabhava</term> (Dual/mutable).
- **Quality:** <term>Krura</term> (Fierce or harsh) and <term>Akrura</term> (Gentle or auspicious).
The letters of the Sanskrit alphabet (<term>Varnas</term>) are distributed across the signs, a system often used to determine the compatibility of names with an individual's birth sign.
</note>

->© Dharmartha Trust. Digitized by eGangotri<-
<note>original: "© Dharmartha Trust. Digitized by eGangotri"</note>
<note>This modern digital watermark credits the Dharmartha Trust as the custodian and eGangotri for the digital preservation of the manuscript.</note>

<summary>This page features a specialized astrological grid called the 'Varnanam Rashichakram,' which maps the Sanskrit alphabet to the twelve zodiac signs. It classifies each sign by gender, mobility, and temperament, serving as a guide for naming conventions and divination.</summary>

<keywords>zodiac chart, Sanskrit alphabet, Vedic astrology, Aries to Pisces, Movable-Fixed-Dual, Fierce and Gentle qualities, Varnanam Rashichakram, Dharmartha Trust</keywords>

<note>The text on this page is extremely faded and consists primarily of mirrored bleed-through (show-through) from the reverse side of the paper. It is largely illegible.</note>

<unclear>
[Approximately 12-15 lines of mirrored <term>Devanagari</term>—the script used for Sanskrit—which are too faint to transcribe or translate accurately.]
</unclear>

<note>
In archival studies, this effect is known as "bleed-through." It occurs when the ink's pigment or binder saturates the paper fibers over centuries, eventually becoming visible on the opposite side. Because the characters are mirrored and overlapping, they do not form a coherent text for the reader on this specific page.
</note>

<summary>This page is largely illegible due to ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the manuscript leaf. It contains no distinct Sanskrit text to translate but serves as evidence of the manuscript's physical age and condition.</summary>

<keywords>illegible, bleed-through, Devanagari script, manuscript preservation, show-through</keywords>

# Now begins the Chart of Lunar Mansion Syllables

<note>original: "अथ नक्षत्र वर्ण चक्रम्" (Atha Nakshatra Varna Chakram). This is a technical table used in Vedic astrology to assign phonetic sounds to the <term>Nakshatras</term> (lunar mansions). Each mansion is divided into four quarters, or "padas," each associated with a specific syllable used for naming children born under that star.</note>

| Lunar Mansion | No. | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 |
| :--- | :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Ashvini | 1 <gloss>1</gloss> | Chu | Che | Cho | La |
| Bharani | 2 <gloss>2</gloss> | Li | Lu | Le | Lo |
| Krittika | 3 <gloss>3</gloss> | A | I | U | E |

| Punarvasu | 7 <gloss>7</gloss> | Ke | Ko | Ha | Hi |
| Pushya | 8 <gloss>8</gloss> | Hu | He | Ho | Da |
| Ashlesha | 9 <gloss>9</gloss> | Di | Du | De | Do |
| Magha | 10 <gloss>10</gloss> | Ma | Mi | Mu | Me |
| Purva Phal<gloss>guni</gloss> | 11 <gloss>11</gloss> | Mo | Ta | Ti | Tu |
| Uttara Phal<gloss>guni</gloss> | 12 <gloss>12</gloss> | Te | To | Pa | Pi |

| Svati | 15 <gloss>15</gloss> | Ru | Re | Ro | Ta |
| Vishakha | 16 <gloss>16</gloss> | Ti | Tu | Te | To |

| Mula | 19 <gloss>19</gloss> | Ye | Yo | Bha | Bhi |
| Purvashadha | 20 <gloss>20</gloss> | Bhu | Dha | Pha | Dha |
| Uttarashadha | 21 <gloss>21</gloss> | Bhe | Bho | Ja | Ji |
| Abhijit <note>An intercalary lunar mansion often excluded from the standard 27-star list.</note> | 22 | Ju | Je | Jo | Kha |
| Shravana | 23 <gloss>22</gloss> | Khi | Khu | Khe | Kho |

| Revati | 28 <gloss>27</gloss> | De | Do | Cha | Chi |

<note>The primary numbers in the second column count 28 mansions including Abhijit. The <gloss> annotations provide the alternate numbering for the standard 27-mansion system, where Abhijit is omitted and Shravana becomes the 22nd star.</note>

<summary>This page contains a "Nakshatra Varna Chakra," a reference table used to determine the auspicious first syllable of a person's name based on the position of the moon at birth. It lists 28 lunar mansions, including the rare intercalary star Abhijit, and maps them to specific phonetic sounds.</summary>
<keywords>Nakshatra, Vedic astrology, lunar mansions, naming syllables, Abhijit, Sanskrit phonetics, horoscopy</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition from the astrological calculations of the previous page to a foundational linguistic exercise. While the previous leaf focused on the specific sounds associated with the stars, this page provides a formal grid of the Sanskrit phonetic system. This suggests the manuscript may have served as a reference manual for a scribe or a student in Kashmir, where the Sharada script was the standard for centuries.</meta>

# The Foundation of Letters: The Syllabary Chart

<note>This page contains what is known in Indian traditions as a <term>Barakhadi</term>. It is a systematic grid used to teach how vowels (the "soul" of the sound) combine with consonants (the "body" of the sound). This chart focuses on the "Velar" consonants—sounds produced at the back of the throat.</note>

| [Column 5] <gloss>Gha</gloss> | [Column 4] <gloss>Ga</gloss> | [Column 3] <gloss>Kha</gloss> | [Column 2] <gloss>Ka</gloss> | [Column 1] <gloss>Vowels</gloss> |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| Gha | Ga | Kha | Ka | A <note>short 'a' as in 'above'</note> |
| Ghā | Gā | Khā | Kā | Ā <note>long 'a' as in 'father'</note> |
| Ghi | Gi | Khi | Ki | I <note>short 'i' as in 'bit'</note> |
| Ghī | Gī | Khī | Kī | Ī <note>long 'i' as in 'beet'</note> |
| Ghu | Gu | Khu | Ku | U <note>short 'u' as in 'put'</note> |
| Ghū | Gū | Khū | Kū | Ū <note>long 'u' as in 'boot'</note> |
| Ghṛ | Gṛ | Khṛ | Kṛ | Ṛ <note>vocalic 'r' as in 'rhythm'</note> |
| Ghṝ | Gṝ | Khṝ | Kṝ | Ṝ <note>long vocalic 'r'</note> |
| Ghḷ | Gḷ | Khḷ | Kḷ | Ḷ <note>vocalic 'l'</note> |
| Ghḹ | Gḹ | Khḹ | Kḹ | Ḹ <note>long vocalic 'l'</note> |
| Ghe | Ge | Khe | Ke | E <note>as in 'play'</note> |
| Ghai | Gai | Khai | Kai | Ai <note>as in 'aisle'</note> |
| Gho | Go | Kho | Ko | O <note>as in 'go'</note> |
| Ghau | Gau | Khau | Kau | Au <note>as in 'house'</note> |
| Ghaṃ | Gaṃ | Khaṃ | Kaṃ | Aṃ <note>the 'Anusvara' or nasal after-sound</note> |
| Ghaḥ | Gaḥ | Khaḥ | Kaḥ | Aḥ <note>the 'Visarga' or breathy aspiration</note> |

<page-num>2</page-num>

<summary>This page presents a foundational Sanskrit syllabary (Barakhadi) in the Sharada script, systematically pairing the sixteen vowels with the first four consonants of the alphabet. It likely served as a pedagogical tool or a reference for scribal training in historical Kashmir.</summary>
<keywords>Sharada script, Sanskrit, Barakhadi, syllabary, velar consonants, phonetics, Kashmir manuscript, educational text</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page follows the previous leaf which detailed the Sanskrit phonetic system (the syllabary). Here, the focus shifts from the basic building blocks of language to the foundational structure of Vedic astrology. The scribe has provided a map of the twelve houses (Bhavas) of a birth chart, illustrating how celestial positions were believed to govern specific parts of the human body and various facets of earthly life.</meta>

# The Twelve Houses
## The Zodiacal Circle from the "Body" to "Expenditure"

<note>The following sections represent the contents of the twelve houses in a traditional North Indian astrological diagram (<term>Kundali</term>). <meta>This page transitions from the mathematical tables of the previous section to the formal opening and introductory commentary of a specific astrological treatise called the 'Panchasvara'. It marks the beginning of the text proper with traditional invocations.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>

<margin>Shri 1</margin> **Om, salutations to the glorious Ganesha!** Having bowed to Heramba <note>A five-headed form of the elephant-god Ganesha, known as the "remover of obstacles."</note>, the destroyer of obstacles, and then to the Voice <note>Saraswati, the goddess of wisdom and speech.</note> and the Sun; meditating upon Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva together with their Shaktis <note>Divine feminine energies or consorts.</note> and the Goddess of All, Kalika; holding her in my heart, I compose this commentary for the enlightenment of the young <note>original: "balabodhana," meaning a simplified explanation for beginners or "children" in the science.</note> and for the joy of all. In this work created by Prajapati, the determination of lifespan is fixed. (1)

At the beginning of this book, in order to complete the work without obstacles, the author speaks an auspicious invocation <term>Mangalacharana: a traditional opening prayer or benediction to invite divine blessings and ensure the success of a literary work.</term> in the <term>Anushtup: a classic Sanskrit poetic meter consisting of four quarters of eight syllables each.</term> meter, which takes the form of a bow to his chosen deity:

**Om, salutations to the glorious Ganesha.**
**Having bowed to my chosen deity, Gopala,** <note>A name for the deity Krishna, particularly as the "protector of cows."</note>
**the family deity, this compendium of texts**
**is being prepared by Shri Prajapati Dasa. (1)**
**Having bowed to the nine planets** <term>Navagraha: the nine celestial bodies influential in Vedic astrology—Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, and the two nodes, Rahu and Ketu.</term>
**and to Goddess Saraswati,**
**and having prostrated before my Teacher,**
**I shall speak a little of this astrological treatise. (2)**

...by me, Shri Prajapati Dasa, a compendium of texts—specifically a collection of the book named <term>Panchasvara: literally "Five Notes/Vowels," an astrological system that uses phonetics and vowels to make predictions.</term>—is being made. This is a thorough determination of auspicious and inauspicious results. This collection of good and bad omens is performed through an investigation into planetary afflictions, starting with the determination of childhood health risks <term>Balarishta: specific planetary combinations thought to indicate potential illness or danger to a child in early life.</term> and lifespan, and following the analysis of the <term>Tripataaki: a "three-bannered" astrological chart or technique used for predicting the events of a year.</term> and the <term>Ketu-pataki</term> and <term>Ketu</term> charts, and by considering the yearly cycles, major planetary periods <term>Dasha</term>, and sub-periods <term>Antardasha</term>. 

It is being performed—having done what? Having bowed to Gopala. What kind of Gopala? The family deity. (1) Now the teacher [speaks of] the obstacles...

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening of the Panchasvara, an astrological manuscript. It contains traditional prayers to Ganesha, Saraswati, and Krishna, and outlines the book's purpose: calculating lifespan and predicting life events using specialized charts and planetary periods.</summary>
<keywords>Ganesha, Prajapati Dasa, Panchasvara, Jyotish, Mangalacharana, Balarishta, Tripataaki, Dasha, Vedic astrology, invocation</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary (Vritti) of the Panchasvara, moving from the general invocation of deities to the specific acknowledgement of the author’s lineage, his sources, and the technical scope of the work. It establishes the author, Prajapati Dasa, as a member of a physician family (Vaidya) and a follower of the classic traditions of Varahamihira.</meta>

...with auspiciousness, he again performs an auspicious invocation in the <term>Anushtup: a classic four-lined poetic meter of eight syllables per line.</term> meter. "To the nine planets" <note>original: "navagrahan"</note> is stated clearly. (2)

Now, he describes his own lineage. Regarding the phrase "By one born of a family of physicians" <note>original: "savaidyakulajatena"</note>: This explanation <term>Parihara: literally a 'remedy' or 'removal,' here meaning a commentary that clears away confusion or doubt.</term> has been composed by me. This "remedy" is a helpful service that removes doubt. Among whom? Among all who wish to know the stars <term>Jyotirvid: an astronomer or astrologer; literally "one who knows the lights."</term>. (3)

Regarding "Varaha": Whatever little is done by me here is done after properly understanding the rules <note>original: "sutra"</note> composed by Varaha <note>Referring to Varahamihira, the 6th-century polymath whose works are foundational to Indian astral sciences.</note> and following them. Therefore, let the astrologers...

->**This explanation has been composed by me, born of a family of physicians.**<-
->**It is for all those who wish to know the stars, and especially for the Brahmins. (3)**<-
->**Whatever I am doing here is according to the rules composed by Varaha.**<-
->**Let the astrologers examine it through a proper consideration of the planets. (4)**<-
->**I shall speak briefly of this perpetual book named "Five Vowels" (Panchasvara),**<-
->**which concerns the mystery of death and is understood in certain regions. (5)**<-

...let them examine the planets through proper consideration. (4) 

Now, he states the name of the book in the Anushtup meter: "Five Vowels." I shall speak of this book named <term>Panchasvara: an astrological system that uses the five primary vowels (a, i, u, e, o) to determine auspiciousness and longevity.</term> after making it brief, having heard it from the mouth of my teacher <note>original: "gurumukhat," emphasizing the oral tradition of knowledge transmission.</note>, and as it is understood in certain regions. What kind of book is it? It "rests on death" <note>original: "nidhana-samshrayam"</note>—meaning it is that which determines the time of death or the end of life. (5)

<summary>In this opening section, the author Prajapati Dasa identifies himself as a member of a Vaidya (physician) lineage and clarifies that his work is a distillation of the teachings of the great astronomer Varahamihira. He introduces the "Panchasvara" system, explaining that its primary purpose is the determination of lifespan and death.</summary>

<keywords>Prajapati Dasa, Vaidya lineage, Varahamihira, Panchasvara, longevity, death, Jyotish, Anushtup meter, Guru-shishya tradition</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of existing astrological practices. While the previous page established the author’s lineage and intent to follow Varahamihira, this section begins a specific polemic against "earlier teachers" who calculate the results of the twelve houses without first considering the lifespan or "arishta" (misfortune/death-signs) of the subject.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>

<margin>Auspicious 2</margin>

Now the Teacher, showing the faults in the works composed by earlier teachers, says:

"By the astrologers..." <note>original: "jyotirvidbhir"</note> Formerly, in the past, a detailed determination was made by all astrologers within the zodiac wheel <term>Rashichakra: the circular chart of the twelve zodiac signs.</term> concerning the twelve houses <term>Bhava: the twelve astrological 'houses' that govern different aspects of life, such as the 1st house (body/self) or the 10th house (career).</term> starting with the house of the Body. In their books, only the results of these houses are established. (6)

Regarding "Duty..." <note>original: "dharma"</note> those of poor intellect <note>original: "kumata-yo"</note> consider duty and actions <note>referring to the 9th and 10th houses</note>, happiness, the sky <note>the 10th house</note>, wives, sons, and so on, in many ways and in vain.

> ->**By all astrologers in ancient times, an extensive determination was made;**<-
> ->**from the Body to Expenditure, arranged in the zodiac wheel. (6)**<-
> ->**Regarding duty, action, happiness, servants, and children;**<-
> ->**there, those of poor intellect describe them in vain. (7)**<-
> ->**Ignoring death and life, they look at the auspicious and inauspicious.**<-
> ->**When one will die by fate, who indeed enjoys the auspicious or inauspicious? (8)**<-

This means they are speaking [predictions]—but after doing what? The meaning is: without having first made a determination or gained knowledge of that misfortune <term>Arishta: specific planetary combinations that portend early death or great calamity.</term> which brings death. (7)

Now the Teacher highlights their fault with the word "Ignoring" <note>original: "apashya"</note>. In that consideration of the twelve houses, having ignored—meaning abandoned—the question of life and death, they speak of auspicious and inauspicious results. When, by fate, the newborn is destined to die, then that which was spoken previously... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence.</note>

<vocab>Astrologers (Jyotirvidbhir), Zodiac wheel (Rashichakra), The Body/1st House (Tanvadi), Twelve Houses (Dvadashabhava), Determination (Nirnaya), Poor intellects (Kumatayo), Duty and Action (Dharmakarma), Ignoring/Discarding (Apashya), Auspicious and Inauspicious (Shubhashubham), Will die (Marishyati), Earlier Teachers (Purvacharya), Books/Treatises (Grantheshu)</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques earlier astrologers for predicting a person's future (wealth, family, career) without first checking if the person will survive infancy or childhood. It argues that predicting "auspicious results" is useless if the subject's lifespan is not first determined through the study of Arishta.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotisha, Bhavas, Arishta, Lifespan, Twelve Houses, Rashichakra, Astrological Critique, Predictions</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument from the previous folio. Having established that predictions regarding career, family, and wealth are useless if the subject does not survive, the author now defines the specific timeframe for assessing "Arishta" (childhood misfortune or early mortality) and introduces the Tripataki Chakra as a primary diagnostic tool.</meta>

...[no one] enjoys the auspicious or inauspicious results. This is the meaning. (8)

### The Purpose of the Treatise

Now, he states the purpose of this scientific text and book. It has been said:

"For indeed, regarding any science or any action, as long as the purpose has not been stated, it will not be accepted by anyone."

Therefore—for that reason—the wise man should investigate the place of longevity <note>original: *ayuh-sthanam*</note> and the place of death with every effort. Just upon the birth of a child, the knower of the time of birth [the astrologer] should consider:

> ->**Therefore, with every effort, one should reflect on the place of longevity;**<-
> ->**Just by the birth of a child, one should reflect on the misfortune of birth. (9)**<-
> ->**Until twenty-four years have passed from the time of birth,**<-
> ->**so long must misfortune be considered; [afterward] one should reflect on a long life. (10)**<-

Just by the birth, one should investigate the misfortune of birth—that is, the misfortune <term>Arishta: a period of planetary ill-will that threatens the life of a newborn or young child.</term> produced by the specific time of birth. (9)

Now he states the duration of this misfortune: "Twenty-four..." and so on. In this regard, for twenty-four years—from the moment of birth until the end of the twenty-fourth year—the astrologer should determine the child's misfortune using the **Tripataki Chakra** <term>Tripataki Chakra: literally "The Three-Bannered Figure," a specialized astrological chart used specifically to determine the health and lifespan of children.</term>. Having understood it thoroughly, he should then speak. After that, he should reflect on the long life <note>longevity exceeding the childhood danger period</note> which will be described hereafter. This is my opinion. (10)

### Alternative Opinions

Now he states another opinion: "Nine-eyes..." <note>original: *navanetrani*</note>. However, some teachers say that from birth until "nine-eyes"—which means twenty-nine years <note>Using the *Bhuta-sankhya* system where 'eyes' represent 2 and 'nine' represents 9; read right-to-left, this denotes 29.</note>—[misfortune must be considered].

<summary>This page establishes the necessity of calculating a child's potential for early mortality (Arishta) before making other predictions, setting the critical period for these checks at twenty-four (or twenty-nine) years using the Tripataki Chakra.</summary>
<keywords>Arishta, Tripataki Chakra, Longevity, Ayuh, Astrology, Childhood Mortality, Twenty-four years, Navanetrani</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for assessing "Arishta" (childhood misfortune). While the previous page mentioned a twenty-four-year period for monitoring a child's health, this page introduces a specific "hidden" period of eleven years and begins the practical construction of the Tripataki Chakra, a diagram used to visualize planetary influences on the newborn.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>

<margin>Folio 3</margin>

...as long as [the child] lives, so long should the hidden form of misfortune be investigated.

### The Construction of the Tripataki Chakra

Now, for the purpose of investigating the **Vedha** <term>Vedha: literally "piercing" or "obstruction"; refers to the specific alignment where a planet exerts a powerful influence (usually negative) on a point in the astrological chart.</term>, he describes the drawing of the **Tripataki Chakra**:

By saying "Vertical," he means one should draw three lines ending towards the East. From the tips of those, coming from two lines each, one should draw six diagonal lines: starting from the Southeast <note>original: *Agneya*</note> and ending in the Northwest <note>original: *Vayavya*</note>, and starting from the Northeast <note>original: *Eishanya*</note> and ending in the Southwest <note>original: *Nairutya*</note>. This is the connection with the following verse. (12)

Regarding the six lines:

> ->**Until eleven years have passed from the time of birth,**<- <note>original: *nava-netrāṇi varṣāṇi* (literally "nine and two years")</note>
> ->**so long must misfortune be investigated in its hidden form, and not otherwise.**<-
> ->**Three vertical lines should be drawn, and three horizontal lines likewise;**<-
> ->**the diagonal influence should be applied in order, two by two, reciprocally.**<-
> ->**Six lines are to be drawn in the determination of the Pataki influence;**<-
> ->**By the influence of the Sun and other planets, the misfortune of birth is established. (13)**<-

For the purpose of determining the influence <note>original: *vedha*</note> in the **Pataki Chakra**, six lines are to be drawn. By means of the influence of the Sun and other planets, the misfortune of birth <note>original: *janmāriṣṭa*</note> for children is established—that is, determined—by **Varaha** <note>Varahamihira, the 6th-century polymath and legendary authority on Indian astrology.</note> and other experts in the science of horoscopy.

It is also said in the *Svarapancashika* <note>An ancient treatise on the "Science of Breath" and its relationship to planetary influences.</note>: "Three horizontal lines and three vertical lines; these six lines are for the purpose of diagnosing diseases occurring at the points where they intersect." (13)

<vocab>त्रिपताकीचक्र (Tripataki Chakra), वेधविचारण (Investigation of planetary influence), जन्मारिष्ट (Birth misfortune/mortality), स्वरपंचाशिका (Svarapancashika), वराह (Varahamihira), आग्नेय (Southeast), ऐशान्या (Northeast), नैर्ऋत्य (Southwest), वायव्य (Northwest)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the critical eleven-year period for "hidden" childhood misfortune and provides geometric instructions for drawing the Tripataki Chakra, a diagnostic grid used to identify planetary obstructions.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing the Tripataki Chakra. While the previous page established the physical geometry of the diagram (the three vertical and three horizontal lines), this page explains where to place specific zodiac signs and planetary values within that grid to determine childhood misfortune.</meta>

Now, he explains how the positions of the planets are determined in the **Tripataki Chakra** <term>Tripataki Chakra: A "three-flag" astrological chart used specifically to predict health and misfortune for children.</term>. He says: "In the distribution of the planets..."

During the initial assignment of the planets, when establishing their positions, one should first write **Aries** <note>original: *Mesha*</note>, the sign of Aries, at the end—at the top peak <note>original: *shringe*</note> of the diagram. 

Now, the intended meaning is this: starting from the south-facing direction, at the ends of the twelve lines that point toward the East, the zodiac signs beginning with Aries should be written. As it is said: "Aries in the middle... following the southern path."

---

<margin>4</margin>

> ->**In the consideration of planetary distribution, one should indicate Aries at the end;**<-
> ->**But in the assignment of the Vedha and the numbers, Aries is certainly placed in the middle.**<-
> ->**The placement of numbers is fixed from Aquarius to Gemini;**<- <note>original: *Kalasha* (Pitcher/Aquarius) to *Dvandva* (Pair/Gemini)</note>
> ->**For the planets in their positions of exaltation, starting with the Sun and others, in order. (15)**<-

---

These [values] are to be assigned in the chart known as the **Tripataki**. Furthermore, at the time of assigning the **Vedha** <term>Vedha: An astrological "piercing" or interference where one planet's position impacts another point in the chart.</term> and the numerical values, one should write the sign of **Aries** in the middle, specifically on the central line. (14)

Now, he describes the placement of numbers within the zodiac circle. By saying "From the pitcher," he means that starting from the sign of **Aquarius** <note>original: *Kumbha*</note> and continuing up to **Gemini** <note>original: *Mithuna*</note>, the distribution and placement of numerical values is performed according to the methods of **Varaha** and others <note>This likely refers to Varahamihira, a legendary 6th-century Indian astronomer and astrologer whose works are foundational to these calculations.</note>.

<summary>This page provides specific rules for labeling the Tripataki Chakra, noting that the position of Aries changes depending on whether one is plotting planets or calculating "Vedha" (interference). It also introduces the sequence for numbering the signs from Aquarius through Gemini.</summary>
<keywords>Tripataki Chakra, Aries, Aquarius, Gemini, planetary exaltation, Varahamihira, Vedha, astrological calculations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the Pañcasvarodaya, transitioning from the physical layout of the chart to the mathematical calculation of a planet's influence. While the previous page established where to place the zodiac signs, this page explains how to interpret the "strength" of planets to predict the specific timing—in days, months, or years—of astrological events.</meta>

-> == Now, this very consideration is as follows: according to the sequence of names, regarding the Southern <unclear>ascent</unclear> and the **Vedha** <-
<term>Vedha: Literally "piercing"; a technical term in Jyotisha (Vedic astrology) referring to an astrological aspect or interference where a planet's position affects another point in the chart.</term>
-> When it occurs, according to the status of the Sun and other planets—by virtue of their being in exaltation, debilitation, or retrograde motion— <-
-> one should specify the portion of the year, or the measurement in years, months, and days, using the numerical values provided here. <-

<margin>
Pañcasvarodaya
4
</margin>

This means it has been stated with certainty. Then, for the Sun and other planets situated in their exalted positions <gloss>meaning the exalted positions described in the book</gloss>, and for those situated in exalted, neutral, or debilitated positions in sequence, the numerical values should be calculated. 

The intended meaning is this: when considering the numerical value of a planet situated in an exalted sign <term>Uchcha: A sign where a planet's influence is at its strongest.</term>, one should combine the numbers from three positions and understand the total as the equivalent number of days. Likewise, for a planet in a neutral position, one should combine the numbers of the two positions it occupies and understand the total as the equivalent number of months. In the same way, for a planet in a debilitated position <term>Neecha: A sign where a planet's influence is at its weakest.</term>, the number assigned to that position itself should be known as the number of years. 

This calculation applies to a **Vedha** caused by a benefic planet <note>original: *shubha-graha*; beneficial planets like Jupiter or Venus</note>; however, for a **Vedha** caused by a malefic planet <note>original: *papa-graha*; harmful planets like Saturn or Mars</note>, it should be understood in the opposite way. Thus it is said, "the strength of the planets should be indicated." This implies that in a balanced assessment, planets with full, medium, or low strength provide results in years, months, or days respectively. Conversely, for benefic planets in auspicious placements, the timing of the results—whether days or months—is determined by the relative difference in their strength. (15)

Now, the **relative strength and weakness**<note>original: "balabala"</note> of the Sun and the other planets is described according to another text.

The domains of the planets, beginning with **Aries**<note>original: "ajadaya", meaning "Aries and the others"</note>, are [the signs of] **Mars, Venus, Mercury, the Moon, the Sun, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Saturn, and Jupiter** respectively. 1. 

When a planet is situated in its **own domain**<note>original: "svakshetra"</note>, its strength is full. It is known to be three-quarters when situated in a **friendly sign**<note>original: "mitrabhe"</note>; half when in a **neutral house**; and one-quarter when situated in an **enemy's house**<term>shatru-griha</term><note>An "enemy's house" is a zodiac sign ruled by a planet that is naturally or functionally hostile to the planet in question.</note>. 2. This aforementioned strength applies to **benefic**<term>saumya</term><note>Benefic planets (Saumya) are considered naturally gentle or auspicious, like Jupiter or a full Moon.</note> planets; for **malefic**<term>krura</term><note>Malefic planets (Krura) are considered "cruel" or harsh, such as Saturn or Mars.</note> planets, the rule should be understood as the reverse.

Now, the specific rule for **malefic planets**<note>original: "papa-graha"</note>:
When situated in an enemy's house, the strength is full; in a neutral residence, it is three-quarters. It is known to be half in a friend's house, and a malefic planet has only one-quarter strength in its own residence. 3. 

Now, the **transit duration through a sign**<term>rashi bhoga</term><note>This refers to the average amount of time a planet takes to move through one zodiac sign (30 degrees).</note>:
One year is declared for **Jupiter**<note>original: "Vrihaspati"</note>; two and a half years for **Saturn**<note>original: "Bhaskare", the son of the Sun.</note>; two and a quarter days for the **Moon**<note>original: "Himatanu", the 'cool-bodied' one.</note>; three fortnights (45 days) for **Mars**<note>original: "Bhumija", the 'son of the Earth'.</note>; twenty-six days for **Venus**<term>danaguru</term><note>Literally the "teacher of the demons" (Asuras).</note>; twenty-two nights for **Mercury**; and for the **Sun**<note>original: "Ravi"</note> six months and a year... <note>The text here becomes fragmented or suggests a different system of calculation for the Sun's transit relative to other factors.</note>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary characteristics, moving from the spatial "Vedha" obstructions to the physical and symbolic qualities of planetary motion and strength. It specifically references the authority of "Bhaskara," likely referring to the famed mathematician-astronomer Bhaskara II (12th century) or an earlier namesake.</meta>

As stated in the **Bhaskara** [text]: 4. 

Now, the characteristics of the rising and other states of the planets are described:

Planets that are freed from the Sun's light begin to **rise**<note>original: "udayante"</note>. When the Sun is in the second sign from a planet, that planet is known to be **fast**<term>shighra</term>. When the Sun is in the third sign, the planet is **moderate**<note>original: "manda", literally "slow," but here representing the middle speed between fast and retrograde</note>. When the Sun is in the fourth sign, the planets are slow. 5.

When the Sun is in the fifth or sixth signs [relative to the planet], the planets become **retrograde**<term>vakra</term><note>Retrograde motion is an optical illusion where a planet appears to move backward in the sky from the perspective of Earth.</note>. When the Sun is in the seventh or eighth signs, they are **extremely retrograde**<note>original: "ativakra"</note>. When the Sun is in the ninth or tenth signs, a **crooked motion**<term>kutila gati</term><note>A transitional phase where the planet's speed is irregular as it prepares to turn direct.</note> arises. 6.

When the Sun is in the eleventh or twelfth signs, the planet attains **speed** once again. In this way, the planets that have moved toward the Sun return to visibility in the world. 7.

### The Consideration of Malefic and Benefic Planets

**Saturn, the Sun, Rahu, Ketu, and Mars** are **malefic**<term>krura</term><note>Literally "cruel" or "harsh" planets. In Vedic astrology, the Sun is considered harsh due to its heat, while Rahu and Ketu are the lunar nodes associated with eclipses.</note>. The remaining are **benefic**<term>shubha</term> planets. **Mercury** joined with a malefic becomes malefic himself; likewise, the **waning Moon** is considered malefic. 8.

Malefic planets in **retrograde** become **extremely malefic**; benefic planets in retrograde become **extremely auspicious**<note>original: "maha shubha"</note>. When situated in their natural states, they act according to their inherent nature. Both benefics and malefics are simply their natural selves when they are **fast-moving**. 9.

A planet in **retrograde** or in its **exaltation**<term>ucca</term> becomes **righteous** and full of **strength**<term>virya</term>. For the degrees behind those positions, the strength is calculated according to the **Rule of Three**<term>trairashika</term><note>A mathematical method of proportion used to find an unknown fourth value based on three known values.</note>. 10.

### The Exaltation Signs of the Sun and Others

Now the **exaltation positions**<note>original: "ucca-sthana"</note> of the Sun and the other planets are told:

**Aries, Taurus, Capricorn, Virgo, Cancer, Pisces, and Libra** are, in order, the **exaltations** for the Sun and the other planets. 11. <note>The order follows the standard sequence: Sun (Aries), Moon (Taurus), Mars (Capricorn), Mercury (Virgo), Jupiter (Cancer), Venus (Pisces), and Saturn (Libra).</note> 

The **debilitation**<term>neecha</term> position is the seventh sign from the exaltation. The place that is fourth from both the exaltation and debilitation is called the **neutral place**<note>original: "samastana"</note>. Thus, the arrangement is fixed in a three-fold wheel: **High (Exalted), Middle (Neutral), and Low (Debilitated)**. 12.

The **strength**<term>bala</term> is full at the degree of exaltation. At the degree of debilitation, the strength is **half**<note>original: "dala"</note>. By means of the **Rule of Three**, the wise should understand the strength for the intermediate degrees. 13.

Having intellectually understood the strengths and the positions of the planets—whether they are **benefic, malefic, retrograde, direct, exalted, or debilitated**—one should then declare the results of the reading. 14.

<summary>This page outlines the relationship between the Sun's position and planetary speed/retrograde motion, classifies planets as malefic or benefic, and defines the signs of exaltation and debilitation used to calculate a planet's strength.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, retrograde motion, Vakra, Krura, Shubha, Exaltation (Ucca), Debilitation (Neecha), Planetary Strength (Bala), Rule of Three (Trairashika), Bhaskara</keywords>

# Obstruction

<margin>Folio 5, Section 1</margin>

When a planet is in **retrograde motion**<term>vakra</term><note>original: "vakragrahe"</note>, its predicted result is **doubled**. When a planet is situated in its **sign of exaltation**<term>svoccha</term><note>The zodiac sign where a planet's influence is most potent.</note>, it gives its **natural** or inherent result. In **fast motion**<term>shighra</term>, it gives its standard result, but when situated in its **sign of debilitation**<term>nicha</term><note>The zodiac sign where a planet is at its weakest.</note>, the result is reduced to **half**. 15.

The planets cast their gaze upon the third, fifth, fourth, and seventh houses from themselves with a gradual increase in the strength of their "quarter-glance." **Saturn**<note>original: "manda", literally "the slow mover"</note>, **Jupiter**<note>original: "guru"</note>, and **Mars**<note>original: "bhumisuta", literally "son of the Earth"</note>, followed by the others, possess **full sight**<note>original: "sampurna-drisho", referring to a full-strength aspect or glance toward specific houses</note> according to their sequence. 16.

The **Sun, Moon, Venus, and Mercury**<note>original: "ravi-indu-sita-saumya"</note> are strong in their eastern glance. **Jupiter**<note>original: "suracharya", the teacher of the gods</note> is strong in a three-quarter glance, and **Mars** is strong in a two-quarter glance. Similarly, **Saturn**<note>original: "shauri"</note> is said to give results through a one-quarter glance. This is the description of planetary glances. 19.

->When a **malefic** planet is **strong**, the affliction is ordained for **only a day**.<-
->When it is of **medium strength**, a **month**; and when **weak**, a **year**.<-
->|| 25 ||<-

Now, the determination of years and days is explained: Regarding the phrase "when strong," when a **malefic planet** causing an **obstruction**<term>vedhakaraka</term><note>A planet that blocks or interferes with the influence of another through its position.</note> is possessed of full strength, a **misfortune**<term>arishta</term><note>An auspicious or harmful period of ill-luck.</note> is ordained for only a single day; this is stated by the sage **Varahamihira** and others. Likewise, when the malefic planet causing the obstruction has medium strength, the misfortune is said to last for exactly one month. In the same way, when the malefic planet causing the obstruction is powerless or **devoid of strength**, the ancient teachers have stated the duration of the effect to be one full year. 25.

<summary>This page outlines how planetary strength and motion (retrograde or fast) multiply astrological results and defines the specific "glances" (aspects) of each planet. It notably details a rule where a stronger malefic planet causes a shorter, more intense obstruction than a weak one.</summary>
<keywords>retrograde, exaltation, debilitation, planetary aspects, Varahamihira, Arishta, Vedhakaraka, planetary strength</keywords>

## Now, the special rule regarding the obstruction by a beneficent planet is stated

Now, the specialty regarding the **obstruction**<term>vedha</term><note>original: "vedhe". In Vedic astrology, Vedha refers to the "obstruction" or interference of one planet's influence by another.</note> of a **beneficent planet**<term>shubha-graha</term><note>original: "shubhagrahasya". Planets like Jupiter, Venus, and a waxing Moon are traditionally considered beneficent.</note> is explained. When an obstruction is created by a beneficent planet that is **endowed with strength**<note>original: "balānvite"</note>:
According to the <gloss>thus</gloss> numerical proportion of its value, one should understand the duration to be a **year**<note>original: "varṣaṃ"</note>. One should predict the **misfortune**<term>arishta</term><note>original: "ariṣṭaṃ". A term for ill-luck, calamity, or a period of physical danger.</note> in terms of years. Again, if the beneficent planet possesses **medium strength**<note>original: "madhyabale"</note>, 
then the misfortune is calculated by a measurement of **months**<note>original: "māsa"</note> equal to the numerical value. Similarly, if the beneficent planet is **weak**<note>original: "dīna"</note> or devoid of strength, it equals one **day**<note>original: "dinam"</note>.

---

When a **beneficent planet** is full of strength, understand the time as a **year**; 
likewise, if it is of **medium strength**, a **month**; 
and if it is **weak**, likewise a **day**. 30. 
If a **malefic planet**<term>papa-graha</term><note>original: "pāpagrahe". Generally refers to Saturn, Mars, Rahu, or Ketu.</note> is joined with the **ascendant**<term>lagna</term><note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note> 
or **glances upon** it, or is in an **enemy** <gloss>similar to</gloss> sign, 
then **death**<note>original: "maraṇāya"</note> is certain for that person on that day. 31.

---

The misfortune should be known by the measure of days. 30. Regarding the **malefic**: 
when a malefic planet is joined with the **birth ascendant**<note>original: "janmalagne"</note>, 
and is further joined by an **enemy** planet or **aspected** by one, 
then the **death** of the child on a day equal to the numerical measure 
is certain; this should be known with conviction. 32.

<summary>This page defines the duration of astrological misfortunes (Vedha) based on planetary strength, ranging from a day for weak planets to a year for strong ones, and provides specific conditions for predicting death.</summary>

# Regarding the Rule of Equality in Planetary Glances

### Determining the Duration of Misfortune

Regarding the word **"Equal"**<note>original: "tulya"</note>: where there is an **equal glance**<term>tulya-drishti</term><note>original: "tulyadṛṣṭir". This refers to a situation where a benefic and a malefic planet exert equal astrological influence (aspect) on a specific point.</note> of both a **benefic** and a **malefic** planet upon the **birth ascendant**<term>janma-lagna</term><note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth; the most important point in a horoscope.</note>, then, having determined the **relative strength**<term>balabala</term> of these two planets, the **misfortune**<term>arishta</term> should be understood by a measurement of **months**<note>original: "māsamānena"</note> according to the numerical value.

In such a case, this is the arrangement: when the **benefic planet** is **strong**<note>original: "valavati"</note> and the **malefic planet** is **weak**<note>original: "nirvale"</note>, the unit of time is known as a **month**. Otherwise, it is calculated in **days**. This is said regarding the conjunction of planets at a particular moment:

> If there is an equal glance, or even if there is an equal **conjunction**<term>yoga</term>,
> then the **month** should be determined by the relative **strength and weakness** of the planets. 32
> If it is glanced at by a benefic, joined with benefics, or located in a **benefic sign**,
> or even if it is glanced at by its own **Lord**<term>svami</term><note>The planet that rules the zodiac sign in question.</note>, then one should understand the duration as a **year**. 33

<insert>
A planet is considered **strong**<note>original: "sabala"</note> if it is **risen**<term>udita</term><note>Visible in the sky and not "combust" or obscured by the Sun's light.</note>, located in its **own house**<term>svagrihastha</term>, in a **friend's house**, in a **friend's divisional chart**<term>varga</term>, or if it receives a **friend's glance**. 35
A planet glanced at by its **strong Lord** and by strong **benefic planets**, and not joined with **malefic planets**, is considered entirely strong. 36
</insert>

The variation between months and other units depends on the degree of strength. 32.
Regarding the phrase **"glanced at by a benefic"**: this means when there is an **obstruction**<term>vedha</term> by a benefic planet, and it is also glanced at by other benefic planets. Furthermore, if the birth ascendant belongs to a benefic planet and is in a favorable position, then the misfortune should be known in terms of a **year** according to the numerical value. Or, if there is an obstruction by a benefic planet and the birth ascendant is **joined with or glanced at by its own Lord**, the calculation should likewise be known in years.

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the duration of astrological misfortunes (arishta) when benefic and malefic planets have equal influence, shifting the timing between days, months, or years based on planetary strength.</summary>
<keywords>arishta, birth ascendant, planetary strength, benefic planets, malefic planets, planetary aspect, vedha, timing of misfortunes</keywords>

# Regarding the Special Influence of Benefic Glances

Thus, the section on the aspects and conjunctions of **auspicious positions**<term>subha-sthana</term> is completed. 33

Now, the author explains a specific rule regarding the influence of the glance of **benefic planets**<term>subha-graha</term><note>A planet like Jupiter or Venus that is naturally inclined to give positive results.</note>. When the **Lord of the Punishment**<term>dandesha</term><note>A specific planet designated in this system as the ruler of a difficult or "punishing" period.</note> is a **malefic planet**<term>papa-graha</term><note>A planet like Mars or Saturn that is naturally inclined to cause challenges.</note> and is also **weakened**<note>original: "kshina"</note>—for instance, because it is in its **sign of debilitation**<term>nicha</term><note>The specific sign of the zodiac where a planet's power is at its lowest.</note> or an enemy's sign—and the **birth ascendant**<term>janma-lagna</term> is joined with a malefic planet, the duration of the crisis is usually understood to be measured in **days**. However, if strong benefic planets glance upon that same ascendant, then one must instead calculate the duration in **years**. 34

> If the "Punishment" is joined with the inauspicious, and the planet is weak and joined with a malefic,
> yet **benefic planets** glance upon it, then one should apply the measurement of **years**. 34
> If **Jupiter**<term>Guru</term> or **Venus**<term>Bhargava</term> are in the **best division**<term>vargottama</term><note>A planet that occupies the same zodiac sign in both the main birth chart and the ninth-part divisional chart, making it exceptionally strong.</note>, or **exalted**<term>tunga</term>, or in a **cardinal house**<term>kendra</term>,
> then even if the malefic is strong, the experience of misfortune does not last for 
->years. 35<-

Now, the author specifically clarifies the duration of **days**. Regarding the **"best division"**<note>original: "vargottama"</note>: if **Jupiter** or **Venus** are in the best division, or in the best division of the **ninth-part chart**<term>navamsha</term><note>A secondary chart used in Indian astrology to determine the hidden strength of planets.</note>, or are **exalted** within their own highest point, and are also located in **cardinal houses**<note>The 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a horoscope, which are considered pillars of strength.</note>, then even if the **Lord of the Punishment** is a powerful and overwhelming **malefic planet** acting as an obstructing factor, the duration of the misfortune does not extend into years. This means the **misfortune**<term>arishta</term> should be understood to occur within a matter of **days**. 35

<summary>This page discusses how to calculate the exact duration of a difficult astrological period (arishta) by weighing the strength of malefic planets against the protective power of Jupiter and Venus. It explains that strong benefic planets can either extend a period into years or, if placed in key positions like the Vargottama or Kendra, can mitigate the duration to just a few days.</summary>
<keywords>arishta, dandesha, benefic planets, malefic planets, Vargottama, Jupiter, Venus, birth ascendant, exaltation, cardinal houses</keywords>

# Calculations for the Duration of Misfortune and its Remedies

Now, the author discusses another rule regarding **astrological obstruction**<term>vedha</term><note>A technical term meaning "piercing" or "interference," where a planet's influence is blocked or modified by the position of another planet.</note> caused by planets situated in three specific **lunar mansions**<term>nakshatra</term>. It is commonly said that if the **birth ascendant**<term>janma-lagna</term><note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the exact moment of birth.</note> is obstructed in this way, the **misfortune**<term>arishta</term> should be understood to last for only a single day. 36

However, the author clarifies: "That is the opinion of others, but it is my view that for an infant or a young child, such a configuration must always be regarded as a significant period of misfortune." 36

Next, the author explains how to avoid or counteract this obstruction through the influence of the **Staff**<term>danda</term><note>Referring back to the 'Lord of Punishment' or 'Staff' mentioned on the previous page; a specific indicator of difficulty in this system.</note> during the **time division** known as **Iryama**<term>iryama</term>. Regarding the influence of a **benefic planet**<term>shubha-graha</term>:

---

> If there is an obstruction in those three constellations, a duration of only one day is prescribed by some. 
> But this is not my opinion; for a child, the misfortune is always a concern. 36
> If the **Staff** is in an auspicious conjunction, and the entire chart is free from **malefic influence**<term>papa-varjita</term>,
> there will be well-being for the child, provided they are surrounded by all **benefic planets**. 37 

---

This means that if the **Staff** is auspiciously placed and there is a total absence of **malefic planets**<term>papa-graha</term><note>Planets like Mars or Saturn that are traditionally associated with challenges.</note>—and specifically if the lords of the **Iryama** period and the planets causing the obstruction are not malefic—and if the **birth ascendant** is further joined with or viewed by **benefic planets**, then one should predict total well-being in every possible way. In such a case, one should not declare it a period of misfortune. 37

<summary>The author debates the "one-day" rule for childhood misfortune, arguing that infants require more careful consideration, while also providing planetary conditions that cancel out negative predictions.</summary>
<keywords>astrological obstruction, birth ascendant, misfortune, childhood health, benefic planets, Staff of Punishment, Iryama period</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on calculating the duration of 'arishta' (misfortune) for a newborn. While the previous page focused on the "Lord of Punishment" or "Staff" (Daṇḍeśa) to determine the length of a crisis, this page details the specific conditions under which these planetary influences become fatal for a child, introducing the "Lord of the Half-Watch" (Ardhayāmeśa).</meta>

# The Fatal Influence of the Lord of the Half-Watch and the Staff

Now, the author discusses a specific rule regarding the **malefic nature**<term>pāpatva</term> of the **Lord of the Half-Watch**<term>Ardhayāmeśa</term><note>The ruler of a specific division of the day or night, used to calculate timing in Vedic astrology.</note> and the **Lord of the Staff**<term>Daṇḍeśa</term>. He says: "the inauspicious" <note>original: "aśubham"</note>. If a **malefic planet**<term>pāpagraha</term><note>In Vedic astrology, planets like Saturn, Mars, or the Sun (in some contexts) that are naturally inclined to produce challenging results.</note> acts as the Lord of the Staff, then "everywhere"—meaning both the **obstruction**<term>vedha</term> and the **half-watch**<term>ardhayāma</term>—are also considered malefic.

If this configuration exists, the text states that "the death of the child follows quickly." What is the implication of this? It means that if the Lord of the Staff does not have a malefic nature, there is only **misfortune**<term>ariṣṭa</term> but not necessarily death. However, if the birth occurs during a half-watch [ruled by a malefic], or if the Lord of the Staff is malefic, or if a malefic planet is in the **ascendant**<term>lagna</term> at birth, then death occurs.

->When there is a conjunction with an inauspicious Staff,<-
->and the chart is devoid of all benefic influences,<-
->the death of the child occurs quickly if accompanied by malefic planets. 38<-

### The Divisions of Time for Day and Night

A rotation of six and five [watches] is prescribed for the day and the night. The lords of these **half-watches**<term>ardhayāma</term> are determined according to the tradition of the **ancient teachers**<term>pūrvācārya</term>. 35

There is no doubt about this. 38 Next, the author names the rulers of these half-watches. He uses the phrase "six and five" <note>original: "ṣaṭ-pañca"</note> because there are six Lords of the Half-Watch during the day and five Lords of the Half-Watch during the night. In this way, the lords of the half-watches are declared by the ancient teachers to be the **Lords of the Day**<term>vāreśa</term><note>The planet that rules the specific day of the week on which the birth occurs (e.g., the Sun for Sunday, the Moon for Monday).</note>. 

As it has been said:
> With a sequence of six divisions in the day and five in the night, the rulers of the half-watches are proclaimed by the wise to begin from the **Lord of the Day** <note>original: "vāreśād"</note>.

<summary>This page explains that if the 'Lord of the Staff' is a malefic planet, it signifies the imminent death of a newborn rather than just a period of illness. It also outlines the system for assigning planetary rulers to the eleven 'half-watches' of a 24-hour period, starting with the planet that rules the day of the week.</summary>

<keywords>Ardhayāmeśa, Daṇḍeśa, malefic planets, infant mortality, Arishta, time divisions, Vāreśa, ancient astrological traditions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining planetary rulers over specific divisions of time. Having established the malefic nature of certain lords in the previous section, the text now provides the specific sequences of planetary lords for the eight divisions (half-watches) of both day and night, starting with Sunday.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<sig>Pt. Sva.</sig>

<margin>Pt. Sva. 12</margin>

The author clarifies that very point: "On the Sun’s day." During the day on Sunday, the Sun <note>original: "raviḥ"</note>—who is the Sun <note>original: "sūryaḥ"</note> himself—is to be understood as the ruler, beginning from his own day. Specifically, on Sunday, the lord of the first **half-watch** <term>yāmārdha</term><note>A time unit of 1.5 hours; there are eight such divisions in a 12-hour day and eight in a 12-hour night.</note> is the Sun <note>original: "arkaḥ"</note>. After that, the lord of the second half-watch is Venus <note>original: "śukraḥ"</note>. Then, the lord of the third half-watch is Mercury <note>original: "budhaḥ"</note>. Following him, the fourth is the Moon <note>original: "induḥ"</note>, the fifth is Saturn <note>original: "śaniḥ"</note>, the sixth is Jupiter <note>original: "guruḥ"</note>, the seventh is Mars <note>original: "bhaumaḥ"</note>, and the eighth is the Lord of the Day, the Sun himself. In this same way, the sequences for Monday and the other days of the week are to be understood. As it is said:

->The Sun, Venus, Mercury, the Moon,<-
->the Sun, Venus, and Mercury;<-
->then the Moon, the Sun, Mercury,<-
->Jupiter, Mars, and the Lord of the Day;<-
->these are the lords of the half-watches during the day.<-

<margin>4.</margin> On the Sun’s night, the Sun is first, then Jupiter <note>original: "vācaspatiḥ"</note>, the Moon <note>original: "candramāḥ"</note>, Venus <note>original: "sitaḥ"</note>, Mars <note>original: "bhaumo"</note>, and the Son of the Day-Lord <note>original: "dineśajo"</note> (Saturn); then Mercury <note>original: "jño"</note>, and finally the Sun <note>original: "arko"</note>. These are the lords of the half-watches at night. 4॥

Mercury <note>original: "jño"</note>, Saturn <note>original: "mando"</note>, Jupiter <note>original: "jīvaḥ"</note>, Mars <note>original: "kujaḥ"</note>, and the Sun <note>original: "raviḥ"</note>. 40॥ Now he describes the sequence of the masters of the half-watches at night: "On the Sun’s night." On Sunday at night, the lord of the first half-watch is the Sun <note>original: "raviḥ"</note>. The second is Jupiter <note>original: "guruḥ"</note>, the third is the Moon <note>original: "śaśī"</note>, the fourth is Venus <note>original: "śukraḥ"</note>, the fifth is Mars <note>original: "bhaumaḥ"</note>, the sixth is the Son of the Day-Lord, Saturn <note>original: "śaniḥ"</note>, the seventh is the Knower, Mercury <note>original: "budhaḥ"</note>, and the eighth is the Sun <note>original: "arkaḥ"</note> himself. Thus, on Monday and the other days as well, the lords of the night half-watches follow a sequence of five-by-five. And so it is said:

->The Sun, Jupiter, the Moon, Venus,<-
->Mars, Saturn, Mercury, and the Sun. 41<-

<note>The term "Yamardha" refers to a "half-watch," a period of approximately 1.5 hours. A "Danda" is a smaller unit of about 24 minutes. In these tables, each of the eight half-watches of the day and night is assigned four planetary "lords" that influence that specific time.</note>

<summary>This page contains systematic astrological tables for the "half-watches" (Yamardhas) of the day and night for Mercury (night), Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn. It lists the four planetary influences governing each of the eight time-divisions for these days.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues from the planetary hour tables of the previous page, shifting from the tabular data to the prose explanation of how these time divisions are calculated and assigned to specific planetary "Lords."</meta>

Now, concerning the <term>Lords of the Time-Divisions</term> <note>original: "Dandesh-as"; literally 'Lords of the Rods,' referring to specific subdivisions of time.</note>. During the day, the desired <term>half-watch</term> <note>original: "Yamarddha"; a period of approximately 1.5 hours.</note> should be divided into four parts. The name for these parts is the "three rods." In that calculation, whoever is the Lord of the Half-Watch is known to be the ruler of the first part—the first Lord of the Time-Division. Then, to determine the second Lord of the Time-Division, one should take the number of the first Lord and halve it. Then, to find the third Lord of the Time-Division, one halves that previous number again. This is the method. For example: on Sunday, the Lord of the first half-watch is the <term>Sun</term> <note>original: "Ravi".</note>; he is also known as the first Lord of the Time-Division. Then, for the second division, half of him is <term>Rahu</term> <note>original: "Rahu"; the ascending lunar node, treated as a shadow planet in Vedic astrology.</note>.

-> The Lord of the Time-Division is halved during the day; in the night, a sequence of six is remembered. <-
-> Thus, the wise should know the Lords of the Time-Divisions, beginning from the Lord of the Half-Watch. <-
<note>Verse 42</note>

Half of Rahu <gloss>added</gloss> is <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Budha".</note>, who is the third Lord of the Time-Division. Half of that Mercury is the <term>Moon</term> <note>original: "Chandra".</note>, who is the fourth Lord of the Time-Division. In this way, after making four equal parts of all the half-watches, the Lords of the Divisions are to be known by this sequence of halving. As it is said in the <term>Varahasutra</term> <note>A foundational text or collection of aphorisms attributed to the sage Varahamihira.</note>:

The half of the Sun is Rahu; half of Rahu is Mercury; half of Mercury is the Moon; and half of the Moon is known to be the <term>Jewel of the Day</term> <note>original: "Divamani"; a poetic synonym for the Sun.</note>. (1)
Half of <term>Mars</term> <note>original: "Bhoma".</note> is likewise the Sun; half of <term>Jupiter</term> <note>original: "Guru".</note> is indeed the Moon; half of <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "Shukra".</note> is known to be <term>Mars</term> <note>original: "Kuja".</note>; and half of <term>Saturn</term> <note>original: "Shani".</note> is the same. (2)

Now, concerning the Lords of the Time-Divisions at night: In the night, there are six...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical and astrological rules for subdividing a "half-watch" (Yamarddha) into smaller units called "Dandesh-as." It cites the Varahasutra to establish a hierarchy of planetary relationships used to determine which planet rules each subdivision.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to calculate the planetary rulers of time subdivisions. While the previous page focused on a "halving" method for daytime divisions, this page introduces a "sequence of six" method used for nighttime divisions, citing the authoritative text Swarapanchashika.</meta>

<margin>Pandit Swa.
14
14</margin>

The six-fold positions of the <term>Lords of the Time-Divisions</term> <note>original: "Dandeshas"; the planetary rulers of specific sub-segments of a watch.</note> should be understood in their proper sequence. If one asks how this is determined, it is explained as follows: On Sunday night, the ruler of the first <term>half-watch</term> <note>original: "Yamarddha"; a period of roughly 90 minutes.</note> is the **Sun** <note>original: "Surya".</note>; he is also to be known as the first Lord of the Time-Division. The sixth planet from him is to be known as the second Lord of the Time-Division. In the same way, the sixth from that planet is the third Lord, and the sixth from that one is the fourth Lord. 

This is the underlying principle: just as the first Lord is the Sun, the sixth from him is **Venus** <note>original: "Shukra".</note>. At night, if the Lord of the daytime half-watch is the Sun, then at night, the Lord of the half-watch division [becomes the starting point]. The Lord of the half-watch during the day becomes the [basis for] the Lord of the divisions at night. When the Moon is the ruler of the daytime half-watch, the Moon also becomes the ruler of the nighttime half-watch division, and then the second Lord of the Time-Division is [determined by the sequence]. Thus, from Venus—

> The Sun, the Son of Danu <note>original: "Danuja"; a name for the shadow planet Rahu.</note>, Mercury, and the Ascendant; the Moon, the Sun, and the Asura-priest <note>original: "Ashurakta/Shukra"; referring to Venus.</note>;
> Mars, the Sun, the Son of Danu, the Knower <note>original: "Jna"; Mercury.</note>, the Moon, the Sun, and the Venusian; Jupiter,
> the Moon, the Sun, and the Daitya-guru <note>original: "Daitya"; Venus.</note>; Venus, Mars, the Sun, and the Daitya-guru; Saturn—
> || Mars, the Sun, and the Daitya-guru attend the fire-like Time-Divisions. 43

—the sixth is **Mercury** <note>original: "Budha".</note>, who is the third Lord of the Time-Division. Likewise, the sixth from Mercury is the **Moon** <note>original: "Chandra".</note>, who is to be known as the fourth Lord of the Time-Division. As it is stated in the <term>Swarapanchashika</term> <note>A classical text on "Svara" or the divination of breath and sound.</note>: 

"The Lords of the Time-Divisions are to be understood by the wise according to the sequence of half-watches during the day. At night, the Lords of the Time-Divisions are those found by counting to the sixth, and then the sixth again, from the ruler of the half-watch." 42 

The author clarifies this further using the **Malini meter** <note>A specific rhythmic structure in Sanskrit poetry consisting of 15 syllables per line.</note> in the verse beginning "Sun, Son of Danu, Mercury, and Ascendant..." By first determining the ruler of the half-watch, one then identifies the daytime Lords of the Time-Divisions in the sequence of Sun, Rahu, Mercury, and so on.

<summary>This page explains the astrological calculation for determining the planetary rulers of nighttime time-divisions (Dandeshas), specifically using a method of counting six planets forward from the current ruler. It cites the Swarapanchashika and provides a mnemonic verse in the Malini meter to help practitioners remember the sequences.</summary>

...they are to be understood. As it is said: "The Sun, the Crested One <note>original: "Shikhi"; usually referring to Ketu, the descending lunar node.</note>, Mercury, the Moon, the Lord of Waters <note>original: "Ap-pati"; Varuna, often associated with the West or specific lunar mansions.</note>, Indra, and the Sun; the Asura-priest <note>original: "Asurakta"; referring to Venus.</note>, Mars, the Son of the Sun <note>original: "Ravija"; Saturn.</note>, the Father of the Danujas <note>original: "Danujatata"; referring to Rahu.</note>, the Stars, and the Immortals <note>original: "Amara"; Jupiter.</note>; Jupiter, the Moon, the Sun, and the Daitya-priest <note>original: "Daitya"; Venus.</note>; Venus, Mars, the Sun, and the Daitya-priest; the Moon, Mars, the Sun, and the Daitya-priest. These are the lords of the day-divisions <note>original: "Dyugana"; the specific segments of time during the daylight hours.</note>." (Verse 43)

Now, [the author] clarifies the <term>Lords of the Divisions</term> <note>original: "Dandeshvaras"; the planetary rulers of the eight subdivisions of the night.</note> for the night. By first identifying the ruler of the first <term>half-watch</term> <note>original: "Yamarddha"; a division of time roughly 90 minutes long.</note>—be it the Sun, Venus, Mercury, or the Moon—the <term>Lords of the Divisions</term> at night are to be known in the order of Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon, and so on.

# Now, here is an example based on actual experience from Samvat 1877, Shaka 1742 <note>These dates correspond to approximately April 1820 CE.</note>

On the 14th day of the bright half of the month of Chaitra, on a Tuesday, at 39 <term>Ghatis</term> <note>A Ghati is a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes.</note> and 20 [palas], the <term>Purva Phalguni</term> lunar mansion was active until 00:23 Ghatis. The <term>Ganda Yoga</term> <note>An astrological alignment often considered unfavorable.</note> lasted for 18:45 Ghatis, in the <term>Gara Karana</term> <note>One of the eleven divisions of a lunar day.</note>. On this day, endowed with these five limbs of the calendar <note>original: "Panchanga"; refers to the five elements: Tithi, Vara, Nakshatra, Yoga, and Karana.</note>, the total duration of daylight was 30:28 Ghatis. 

At that time, the Sun had progressed 17 degrees into the sign of Pisces <note>original: "Minarka"; the Sun in the zodiac sign of Meena (Pisces).</note>. On that day, at 4:00 Ghatis past the rising of the Sun's orb <note>original: "Martanda-mandala"; referring to sunrise.</note>, a certain boy was born. 

In this boy's birth chart <note>original: "Janma Lagna"; the sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note>, there is an <term>affliction</term> <note>original: "Vedha"; a technical term for a planetary "piercing" or obstruction that influences an astrological point.</note> caused by the Sun, Mercury, Saturn, Rahu, and Ketu. Furthermore, at the time of his birth, the Sun was the ruler of the <term>half-watch</term> <note>original: "Ardhayama"; a 90-minute time division.</note> and was also the <term>Lord of the Division</term> <note>original: "Dandesha"; the specific planetary ruler of that time segment.</note>. 

Therefore, because of this abundance of ill-omens <note>original: "Arishta"; a term for astrological portents of misfortune or early death for a child.</note>, it was determined that the boy's death was a certainty. One might ask: "How is this abundance of ill-omens to be understood?" 

On this point, the Teacher has stated that an affliction by <term>malefic planets</term> <note>original: "Papa-vedha"; planets like Saturn or Rahu considered naturally challenging.</note> constitutes an ill-omen. It is likewise stated that misfortune occurs when both the ruler of the half-watch and the Lord of the Division are malefic. In this specific case, there is an affliction by *many* malefic planets. Furthermore, the Sun is among those causing the affliction, and it is the Sun itself who acts as the ruler of the half-watch and the Lord of the Division. By this, the abundance of ill-omens is established. 

Thus, wherever a malefic planet becomes the ruler of the half-watch or the Lord of the Division and simultaneously creates an affliction, a specific misfortune must be predicted. In such matters of misfortune, the strength of the Lord of the Division should be considered superior <note>original: "Pravalyam"; meaning of greater weight or power in the calculation.</note> to that of the ruler of the half-watch. According to the ancient source text...

<summary>This page provides a practical astrological case study from 1820 CE, demonstrating how multiple malefic planetary "afflictions" (Vedha) occurring during a specific time division led to the prediction of a newborn's death.</summary>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological analysis of the birth chart established on page 15. While the previous page provided the visual diagrams (kundalis) for a birth in April 1820, this page focuses on the mathematical calculation of the child's lifespan, specifically identifying periods of "Arishta" (grave misfortune or threat to life) based on planetary strengths and aspects.</meta>

<margin>
P.S.
16
16
</margin>

When the <term>Danda-esha</term> <note>original: "danda-esha"; literally 'Lord of the Rod' or 'Lord of Punishment,' a specific planetary ruler used to determine the timing of death or punishment.</note> is a <term>Papa-graha</term> <note>original: "papa-graha"; a malefic or "cruel" planet like Saturn or Mars.</note>, death is declared. Therefore, even if the Lord of the Rod is powerful, if it is situated in certain zodiac signs, there may be only <term>Arishta</term> <note>An astrological term for a period of misfortune, illness, or a "stroke of fate" that threatens life but may not result in death.</note> rather than death itself. 

Similarly, in the absence of a <term>Vedha</term> <note>original: "vedha"; an 'obstruction' or 'piercing' aspect where one planet's influence blocks another's.</note>, even if the Lord of the Rod is malefic, the person may live after experiencing the affliction. In the case of this child, since it is known that death will occur due to the abundance of afflictions, the specific time [of death] is now being determined. 

In this analysis, all the planets causing the "obstruction" are cruel and strong. Therefore, only months corresponding to the sign of <term>Mesha</term> <note>original: "mesha"; the zodiac sign Aries.</note> are obtained [in the calculation], rather than years or additional months. In this consideration, the <term>Janma-lagna</term> <note>original: "janma-lagna"; the ascendant or the sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note> is named Aries; there, the numerical value of the three positions is sixteen (16). Thus, sixteen (16) months were determined.

Furthermore, there is an "attack" <note>An "attack" or occupancy refers to a planet physically sitting in a specific house or sign.</note> by <term>Bhrigu</term> <note>original: "bhrigu"; another name for the planet Venus (Shukra).</note> in the birth ascendant. The Teacher has stated that if the Lord of the Rod is benefic, and the ascendant is associated with a benefic planet and free from malefic obstructions, then well-being is predicted. But here, although the benefic planet Venus is occupying the ascendant, there is a total absence of well-being [due to the surrounding malefic influences]. 

However, because the occupying Venus is strong, the months are doubled; thus, thirty-two (32) months are obtained. Furthermore, there is a "quarter-strength" aspect <note>original: "pada-mita drishti"; a glance or astrological aspect that carries only 1/4th of the planet's full power.</note> of a weak <term>Guru</term> <note>original: "guru"; the planet Jupiter.</note> upon the ascendant. Because Jupiter is very weak, from the four points (4) of the position situated near the middle of the three numerical values, only one month is obtained because the aspect is only a quarter...

<summary>This page provides a technical calculation of a child's lifespan, determining that the child will likely live for approximately 33 months (32 from a strong Venus and 1 from a weak Jupiter) before succumbing to malefic astrological "afflictions."</summary>
<keywords>Arishta, Danda-esha, Mesha, Aries, Venus, Bhrigu, Jupiter, Guru, Janma-lagna, astrological calculation, lifespan, Sanskrit manuscript</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific astrological case study from page 15, moving from the general identification of a life-threatening period (Arishta) to a precise calculation of when death will occur. It resolves a technical contradiction between different scriptural rules regarding whether the child's lifespan should be measured in days or months.</meta>

<insert>Not indeed</insert> is it the case that death is only possible in the thirty-second month. Based on the scriptural statement, "when a <term>Papa-graha</term> <note>original: "papa-graha"; a malefic or 'cruel' planet such as Saturn or Mars.</note> is strong, a period of only a day is prescribed," why then did death not occur after 32 days? 

It is because of the strength of the Sun, and because it is joined with Saturn and Rahu, and because Mercury—being joined with these malefics—also acts as a malefic. 

On this point, it is said: "If there is an equal aspect or an equal conjunction, then the period should be assigned in months, according to the relative strength and weakness of the planets." 

Following this statement, because of the aspects of Jupiter and the Moon, and the conjunction with Venus, the malefic planets possess only medium strength. Therefore, the calculation results in months. Thus, death is possible within a month. On which specific day will it occur? 

In this month, when the Moon forms a conjunction with a <term>Vedhakaraka</term> <note>original: "vedhakaraka"; an 'obstructing' or 'piercing' planet that blocks the life-force of another.</note> planet, or when it causes an obstruction while situated in the signs of <term>Karka</term> <note>original: "karka"; the zodiac sign Cancer.</note> or <term>Tula</term> <note>original: "tula"; the zodiac sign Libra.</note>, then the time of death is certain. 

As it is stated in the <term>Narapati</term> <note>original: "Narapati"; referring to the *Narapati-Jayacharya Vidagdha-Madhaviya*, a standard 12th-century Sanskrit text on astrology and omens.</note>: "On the day that [a planet] obstructs the Moon, on that day auspicious or inauspicious results occur." 

Therefore, for this child, as the Moon reached the <term>Lagna</term> <note>original: "lagna"; the ascendant or the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at birth.</note> and received the obstruction, death occurred; thus, the proof of the calculation is established. In this very manner, wherever a possibility of <term>Arishta</term> <note>original: "arishta"; a fatal misfortune or stroke of bad luck.</note> exists in a child’s birth ascendant, death should be observed. 

The determination of that time must always be understood through the relative strength, weakness, and combustion <note>The 'setting' or 'astamaya' of a planet when it gets too close to the Sun and loses its power.</note> of the obstructing planets, the attacking planets, and the auspicious and inauspicious planets, as determined by their aspects and other conditions.

->Thus ends the example, number 44.<-

->One should say the Ascendant <note>original: "Lagna"; the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of birth.</note> originating from the body and birth is situated on the middle line.<-

One should write the birth ascendant, which describes the form and color, on the middle line [of the diagram]. Then, a calculation should be performed starting from the birth year up to the current year. In that context, when the total number of past years is divided by nine, the Moon should be written in the sign corresponding to the remainder, counting from the sign where the Moon was at birth. Again, when that same number [of years] is divided by six, Mars and the "King" should be written in the sign corresponding to the remainder, counting from the birth position. By the term <term>rajna</term> <note>literally "king" or "ruler."</note>, the inclusion of Ketu <note>The descending lunar node, considered a "shadow planet" in Indian astrology.</note> is also intended. || 47 ||
